========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.

And it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. Then I have to sort of pull myself back and remind myself where I am.

More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.\n\nAnd it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.

I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. I just can’t stand seeing it — the curb, the crossing, the traffic moving through like nothing ever happened. It feels unbearable, like my body knows where I am before my mind has even caught up.

And it isn’t only the place itself. I’ve stopped signing up for most tours and public safety meetings, things I used to be willing to attend, because those conversations always seem to drift back toward what happened there. Someone brings up pedestrian safety, or that particular area, or “near misses,” and suddenly I’m trapped listening to people discuss it like it’s a topic on an agenda. I just can’t bear it. So I avoid them now, mostly. I tell myself I’m just not interested, but really I know why.

I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.\n\nI go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve paid attention to it in a very organized way, if that makes sense. Some nights I sleep, some nights I don’t feel like I’ve slept well, but I don’t know that I could tell you it’s a definite pattern or exactly what’s causing it.

At my age, sleep can be odd anyway, you know — waking up here and there, needing to get comfortable, listening to the house at night. So I hesitate to say, “Yes, this is a major problem,” because I’m not sure. I just know that nights can feel long sometimes.

I’m not really sure. I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.

I haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.

I used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.

Now I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.

They’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.

And that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what brings me in is that I just haven’t felt right for a long time. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve been carrying around this awful mix of feelings — guilt, and fear, and anger — and it’s just there all the time. People kept saying it would ease up, that time would soften it, and I kept waiting for that to happen, but it hasn’t, not really. It feels like it’s settled into me somehow, and I’m tired of living with it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your friend's death or have memories of it that feel very vivid?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.\n\nAnd it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. Then I have to sort of pull myself back and remind myself where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or people because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.\n\nI go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. I just can’t stand seeing it — the curb, the crossing, the traffic moving through like nothing ever happened. It feels unbearable, like my body knows where I am before my mind has even caught up.\n\nAnd it isn’t only the place itself. I’ve stopped signing up for most tours and public safety meetings, things I used to be willing to attend, because those conversations always seem to drift back toward what happened there. Someone brings up pedestrian safety, or that particular area, or “near misses,” and suddenly I’m trapped listening to people discuss it like it’s a topic on an agenda. I just can’t bear it. So I avoid them now, mostly. I tell myself I’m just not interested, but really I know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve paid attention to it in a very organized way, if that makes sense. Some nights I sleep, some nights I don’t feel like I’ve slept well, but I don’t know that I could tell you it’s a definite pattern or exactly what’s causing it.\n\nAt my age, sleep can be odd anyway, you know — waking up here and there, needing to get comfortable, listening to the house at night. So I hesitate to say, “Yes, this is a major problem,” because I’m not sure. I just know that nights can feel long sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.\n\nI haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.

People kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.

And the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.

It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.

I used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.

And socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.

So yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.

I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.\n\nI haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.\n\nI used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.\n\nAnd socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most, because those weren’t just hobbies to me. They were the things that made my days feel like mine.

I used to sit in the winter with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan out my garden beds — where the tomatoes would go, which flowers might do better in the shade, what I’d try differently from the year before. I could spend hours on that and feel completely absorbed. And quilting with the ladies, that was always such a comfort. We’d talk and sew and laugh about little mistakes, or someone would bring cookies, and it felt warm and familiar.

There were theater outings too, and I used to look forward to those so much — getting dressed, riding over with the group, talking about the play afterward. And book club… oh, I used to argue happily at book club. Not angrily, just really getting into it, defending a character or saying the ending didn’t work, and I loved that.

Now I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like I’m looking at someone else’s life. I know they used to matter to me. I remember that they did. But when the day comes, I just feel… almost nothing. Not excitement, not anticipation. More like, “That belongs to the woman I used to be,” and I don’t quite know how to get back to her.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what brings me in is that I just haven’t felt right for a long time. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve been carrying around this awful mix of feelings — guilt, and fear, and anger — and it’s just there all the time. People kept saying it would ease up, that time would soften it, and I kept waiting for that to happen, but it hasn’t, not really. It feels like it’s settled into me somehow, and I’m tired of living with it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your friend's death or have memories of it that feel very vivid?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.\n\nAnd it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. Then I have to sort of pull myself back and remind myself where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or people because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.\n\nI go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. I just can’t stand seeing it — the curb, the crossing, the traffic moving through like nothing ever happened. It feels unbearable, like my body knows where I am before my mind has even caught up.\n\nAnd it isn’t only the place itself. I’ve stopped signing up for most tours and public safety meetings, things I used to be willing to attend, because those conversations always seem to drift back toward what happened there. Someone brings up pedestrian safety, or that particular area, or “near misses,” and suddenly I’m trapped listening to people discuss it like it’s a topic on an agenda. I just can’t bear it. So I avoid them now, mostly. I tell myself I’m just not interested, but really I know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve paid attention to it in a very organized way, if that makes sense. Some nights I sleep, some nights I don’t feel like I’ve slept well, but I don’t know that I could tell you it’s a definite pattern or exactly what’s causing it.\n\nAt my age, sleep can be odd anyway, you know — waking up here and there, needing to get comfortable, listening to the house at night. So I hesitate to say, “Yes, this is a major problem,” because I’m not sure. I just know that nights can feel long sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.\n\nI haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.\n\nI used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.\n\nAnd socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to find important or enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most, because those weren’t just hobbies to me. They were the things that made my days feel like mine.\n\nI used to sit in the winter with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan out my garden beds — where the tomatoes would go, which flowers might do better in the shade, what I’d try differently from the year before. I could spend hours on that and feel completely absorbed. And quilting with the ladies, that was always such a comfort. We’d talk and sew and laugh about little mistakes, or someone would bring cookies, and it felt warm and familiar.\n\nThere were theater outings too, and I used to look forward to those so much — getting dressed, riding over with the group, talking about the play afterward. And book club… oh, I used to argue happily at book club. Not angrily, just really getting into it, defending a character or saying the ending didn’t work, and I loved that.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like I’m looking at someone else’s life. I know they used to matter to me. I remember that they did. But when the day comes, I just feel… almost nothing. Not excitement, not anticipation. More like, “That belongs to the woman I used to be,” and I don’t quite know how to get back to her.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

volunteer budgets because I cannot keep up reliably, and I have pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to structure my week.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour about two years and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S074\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.\n\nI used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.\n\nAnd socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to find important or enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most, because those weren’t just hobbies to me. They were the things that made my days feel like mine.\n\nI used to sit in the winter with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan out my garden beds — where the tomatoes would go, which flowers might do better in the shade, what I’d try differently from the year before. I could spend hours on that and feel completely absorbed. And quilting with the ladies, that was always such a comfort. We’d talk and sew and laugh about little mistakes, or someone would bring cookies, and it felt warm and familiar.\n\nThere were theater outings too, and I used to look forward to those so much — getting dressed, riding over with the group, talking about the play afterward. And book club… oh, I used to argue happily at book club. Not angrily, just really getting into it, defending a character or saying the ending didn’t work, and I loved that.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like I’m looking at someone else’s life. I know they used to matter to me. I remember that they did. But when the day comes, I just feel… almost nothing. Not excitement, not anticipation. More like, “That belongs to the woman I used to be,” and I don’t quite know how to get back to her.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Especially near traffic. That’s where I notice it the most.

If I’m walking anywhere close to a road, or even standing near a curb, I feel like I have to watch everything. Every truck that goes by, every car turning, every person stepping off the sidewalk — I’m tracking all of it. I don’t just glance; I sort of… scan. I look at the tires, the speed, whether the driver seems to see the pedestrian, whether someone is too close to the curb. It’s like my body believes it’s my job to catch danger before it happens again.

And I know that sounds excessive. I can hear myself saying it and think, “Well, of course you can’t control all of that.” But when I’m there, in the moment, it doesn’t feel optional. It feels like if I stop watching, even for a second, I’ve failed somehow. So yes, I’m very much on the lookout, particularly around traffic.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what brings me in is that I just haven’t felt right for a long time. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve been carrying around this awful mix of feelings — guilt, and fear, and anger — and it’s just there all the time. People kept saying it would ease up, that time would soften it, and I kept waiting for that to happen, but it hasn’t, not really. It feels like it’s settled into me somehow, and I’m tired of living with it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your friend's death or have memories of it that feel very vivid?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.\n\nAnd it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. Then I have to sort of pull myself back and remind myself where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or people because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.\n\nI go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. I just can’t stand seeing it — the curb, the crossing, the traffic moving through like nothing ever happened. It feels unbearable, like my body knows where I am before my mind has even caught up.\n\nAnd it isn’t only the place itself. I’ve stopped signing up for most tours and public safety meetings, things I used to be willing to attend, because those conversations always seem to drift back toward what happened there. Someone brings up pedestrian safety, or that particular area, or “near misses,” and suddenly I’m trapped listening to people discuss it like it’s a topic on an agenda. I just can’t bear it. So I avoid them now, mostly. I tell myself I’m just not interested, but really I know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve paid attention to it in a very organized way, if that makes sense. Some nights I sleep, some nights I don’t feel like I’ve slept well, but I don’t know that I could tell you it’s a definite pattern or exactly what’s causing it.\n\nAt my age, sleep can be odd anyway, you know — waking up here and there, needing to get comfortable, listening to the house at night. So I hesitate to say, “Yes, this is a major problem,” because I’m not sure. I just know that nights can feel long sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.\n\nI haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.\n\nI used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.\n\nAnd socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to find important or enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most, because those weren’t just hobbies to me. They were the things that made my days feel like mine.\n\nI used to sit in the winter with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan out my garden beds — where the tomatoes would go, which flowers might do better in the shade, what I’d try differently from the year before. I could spend hours on that and feel completely absorbed. And quilting with the ladies, that was always such a comfort. We’d talk and sew and laugh about little mistakes, or someone would bring cookies, and it felt warm and familiar.\n\nThere were theater outings too, and I used to look forward to those so much — getting dressed, riding over with the group, talking about the play afterward. And book club… oh, I used to argue happily at book club. Not angrily, just really getting into it, defending a character or saying the ending didn’t work, and I loved that.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like I’m looking at someone else’s life. I know they used to matter to me. I remember that they did. But when the day comes, I just feel… almost nothing. Not excitement, not anticipation. More like, “That belongs to the woman I used to be,” and I don’t quite know how to get back to her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially near traffic. That’s where I notice it the most.\n\nIf I’m walking anywhere close to a road, or even standing near a curb, I feel like I have to watch everything. Every truck that goes by, every car turning, every person stepping off the sidewalk — I’m tracking all of it. I don’t just glance; I sort of… scan. I look at the tires, the speed, whether the driver seems to see the pedestrian, whether someone is too close to the curb. It’s like my body believes it’s my job to catch danger before it happens again.\n\nAnd I know that sounds excessive. I can hear myself saying it and think, “Well, of course you can’t control all of that.” But when I’m there, in the moment, it doesn’t feel optional. It feels like if I stop watching, even for a second, I’ve failed somehow. So yes, I’m very much on the lookout, particularly around traffic.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it has. I’m not working now, so it isn’t a job in the usual sense, but it has interfered with the responsibilities I had taken on.

At the senior center, I used to help organize programs and coordinate things, and I also helped with some of the volunteer budgets. Those are the kinds of tasks where you really do have to be steady — remember what you promised, follow up, keep track of numbers and dates, answer people when they’re counting on you. And I just haven’t been able to keep up with that reliably anymore. I’d start something and then feel overwhelmed by it, or I’d worry I wasn’t doing it carefully enough, and eventually I had to step back.

That was very hard for me, because I’ve always been someone who liked being useful. I don’t like feeling as if people can’t count on me.

And at home, or just in my ordinary week, I’ve pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to give my days structure. They used to keep me moving — I had places to be, people expecting me, things on the calendar. Now I let more of it slide. So yes, it’s affected my ability to manage the life I used to manage.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose… what brings me in is that I just haven’t felt right for a long time. Ever since my friend was killed, I’ve been carrying around this awful mix of feelings — guilt, and fear, and anger — and it’s just there all the time. People kept saying it would ease up, that time would soften it, and I kept waiting for that to happen, but it hasn’t, not really. It feels like it’s settled into me somehow, and I’m tired of living with it like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about your friend's death or have memories of it that feel very vivid?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, really. It doesn’t always come as a thought I choose to have — it’s more like something in the world catches me off guard. A crosswalk signal changing, or the low growl of a truck going by, or even seeing one of those walking-tour maps folded out in someone’s hands… suddenly the whole thing is right back in my head.\n\nAnd it’s so sharp. It isn’t just “remembering,” exactly. I can see my friend stepping off the curb, like it’s happening again before I can stop it. It comes in so fast, and for a moment I’m not just thinking about it — I’m there with it. Then I have to sort of pull myself back and remind myself where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places, activities, or people because they remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I could say I don’t, because it makes me feel a little foolish at my age, rearranging my life around a street corner, but I absolutely do.\n\nI go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it’s inconvenient, even if it adds time or means I have to explain why I’m going some odd way. I just can’t stand seeing it — the curb, the crossing, the traffic moving through like nothing ever happened. It feels unbearable, like my body knows where I am before my mind has even caught up.\n\nAnd it isn’t only the place itself. I’ve stopped signing up for most tours and public safety meetings, things I used to be willing to attend, because those conversations always seem to drift back toward what happened there. Someone brings up pedestrian safety, or that particular area, or “near misses,” and suddenly I’m trapped listening to people discuss it like it’s a topic on an agenda. I just can’t bear it. So I avoid them now, mostly. I tell myself I’m just not interested, but really I know why.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sleeping at night?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that cleanly. I wouldn’t say I’ve paid attention to it in a very organized way, if that makes sense. Some nights I sleep, some nights I don’t feel like I’ve slept well, but I don’t know that I could tell you it’s a definite pattern or exactly what’s causing it.\n\nAt my age, sleep can be odd anyway, you know — waking up here and there, needing to get comfortable, listening to the house at night. So I hesitate to say, “Yes, this is a major problem,” because I’m not sure. I just know that nights can feel long sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself being easily startled by small things, like sudden noises?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do startle sometimes if something is very loud or right behind me, but I don’t know that I’d say it happens often, or more than it would for anyone else.\n\nI haven’t kept track of it, honestly. If a dish drops or a car horn goes off suddenly, I might jump, but then again most people would, wouldn’t they? So… I don’t think I can confidently say that’s been a big thing for me. It’s not something I’ve particularly noticed as a pattern.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. More than I like to admit, honestly, because those things used to be such a big part of how I recognized myself.\n\nI used to sit with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan my garden beds like it was some grand architectural project — what would go where, which colors would come up first, how the borders would look in June versus September. And quilting with the ladies, that used to be a real pleasure. We’d talk and fuss over fabric and laugh about mistakes. I used to go on theater outings, too, and I could get so absorbed in a play. And book club — I wasn’t quiet there, I’ll tell you that. I used to argue happily about characters and endings and whether an author had earned something or not.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like… I know they’re mine, technically, but they feel like they belong to someone I used to be. I look at them and I don’t feel anticipation. Sometimes I don’t feel much of anything at all. Not dread exactly, just this blankness, like I can’t quite reach the part of me that used to care.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've pulled back from social interactions or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. It’s not always that I refuse invitations outright, though sometimes I do. It’s more that even when I’m physically there, sitting with people I’ve known for years — at the senior center, for instance — I don’t feel quite with them.\n\nThey’ll be talking about their grandchildren, or someone’s recipe, or who’s going where for the holidays, and I can see their mouths moving and I know these are familiar people, kind people, people I used to feel comfortable with. But it feels like they’re all on one side of a window, and I’m on the other side looking in. I can hear them, I can smile at the right places sometimes, but there’s this separation. Like I can’t quite step into the room emotionally, even though I’m sitting right there in the chair.\n\nAnd that’s been… lonely, in a strange way, because I’m not literally alone when it happens. I’m surrounded by people, and still I feel apart from them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or worthless about something related to your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… guilty, yes. Worthless is harder for me to say, exactly, but there’s been this terrible heaviness since my friend was killed. It isn’t just sadness. It’s this mixture of guilt and fear and anger that seems to have settled into me and stayed there.\n\nPeople kept saying, especially early on, that time would make it easier, that the sharp edges would soften. And maybe some things have changed, but that feeling has not really eased the way everyone seemed to think it would. I keep turning it over in my mind — what I should have done, what I didn’t do, whether I missed something, whether I could have somehow changed the outcome. I know, logically, that may not be fair to myself, but emotionally it doesn’t let go.\n\nAnd the anger is there too, which makes me feel ashamed sometimes. I don’t like feeling that much anger. I don’t like that it sits right alongside the guilt. It’s like I can’t find one clean feeling about it — it’s all tangled together, and I’ve been living with that tangle for a long time now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your daily life or relationships since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. Quite a bit, actually. I don’t think I understood at first how much it was spilling over into ordinary life, because I kept telling myself I was just “having a hard time” and that I’d catch up eventually. But I haven’t really caught up.\n\nI used to be very involved at the senior center — not just attending things, but organizing programs, helping coordinate activities, keeping track of little details that honestly I used to be proud of being good at. I also helped manage some of the volunteer budgets, and that requires being reliable, remembering what needs doing, following through when you say you’ll follow through. And I’ve had to step back from that. I hated admitting it, because people depended on me, and I was always someone who could be depended on. But I just couldn’t keep up consistently anymore. I’d look at what needed to be done and feel like I couldn’t quite manage it the way I used to.\n\nAnd socially, yes, I’ve pulled away. The clubs and activities that used to structure my whole week — they gave me places to be, people to see, a reason to get dressed and get out — I’ve drifted back from those too. Sometimes I still think, “Oh, I should go,” but then I don’t, or I go less often, or I don’t really participate the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it has changed my daily life. It’s made everything smaller, I suppose. My week used to have shape to it, and now there are too many empty spaces where those things used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you used to find important or enjoyable since your friend passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I have. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most, because those weren’t just hobbies to me. They were the things that made my days feel like mine.\n\nI used to sit in the winter with seed catalogs and graph paper and plan out my garden beds — where the tomatoes would go, which flowers might do better in the shade, what I’d try differently from the year before. I could spend hours on that and feel completely absorbed. And quilting with the ladies, that was always such a comfort. We’d talk and sew and laugh about little mistakes, or someone would bring cookies, and it felt warm and familiar.\n\nThere were theater outings too, and I used to look forward to those so much — getting dressed, riding over with the group, talking about the play afterward. And book club… oh, I used to argue happily at book club. Not angrily, just really getting into it, defending a character or saying the ending didn’t work, and I loved that.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar, and it’s like I’m looking at someone else’s life. I know they used to matter to me. I remember that they did. But when the day comes, I just feel… almost nothing. Not excitement, not anticipation. More like, “That belongs to the woman I used to be,” and I don’t quite know how to get back to her.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers?\n\nPatient: Yes. Especially near traffic. That’s where I notice it the most.\n\nIf I’m walking anywhere close to a road, or even standing near a curb, I feel like I have to watch everything. Every truck that goes by, every car turning, every person stepping off the sidewalk — I’m tracking all of it. I don’t just glance; I sort of… scan. I look at the tires, the speed, whether the driver seems to see the pedestrian, whether someone is too close to the curb. It’s like my body believes it’s my job to catch danger before it happens again.\n\nAnd I know that sounds excessive. I can hear myself saying it and think, “Well, of course you can’t control all of that.” But when I’m there, in the moment, it doesn’t feel optional. It feels like if I stop watching, even for a second, I’ve failed somehow. So yes, I’m very much on the lookout, particularly around traffic.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like your reactions to your friend's death have interfered with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working now, so it isn’t a job in the usual sense, but it has interfered with the responsibilities I had taken on.\n\nAt the senior center, I used to help organize programs and coordinate things, and I also helped with some of the volunteer budgets. Those are the kinds of tasks where you really do have to be steady — remember what you promised, follow up, keep track of numbers and dates, answer people when they’re counting on you. And I just haven’t been able to keep up with that reliably anymore. I’d start something and then feel overwhelmed by it, or I’d worry I wasn’t doing it carefully enough, and eventually I had to step back.\n\nThat was very hard for me, because I’ve always been someone who liked being useful. I don’t like feeling as if people can’t count on me.\n\nAnd at home, or just in my ordinary week, I’ve pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to give my days structure. They used to keep me moving — I had places to be, people expecting me, things on the calendar. Now I let more of it slide. So yes, it’s affected my ability to manage the life I used to manage.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t like feeling as if people can’t count on me.\n\nAnd at home, or just in my ordinary week, I’ve pulled away from the clubs and activities that used to give my days structure. They used to keep me moving — I had places to be, people expecting me, things on the calendar. Now I let more of it slide. So yes, it’s affected my ability to manage the life I used to manage.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like your reactions to your friend's death have caused significant problems in your relationships with family or friends?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it has. Not always in a dramatic way, not like I’m having arguments with everyone, but it’s changed how I am with people.

Even when I’m sitting with people I’ve known for years at the senior center — people I used to feel perfectly comfortable with — I feel separated from them now. They’ll be talking about ordinary things, who’s bringing what to the next event, someone’s granddaughter, a recipe, the weather, all the usual little bits of conversation that used to make me feel included. And I’ll be sitting right there, nodding, maybe even smiling at the right moments, but inside it feels like they’re on one side of a window and I’m on the other.

It’s a strange feeling, because they haven’t done anything wrong. They’re kind to me. They try. But I don’t feel fully with them anymore. I feel as if there’s this pane of glass between us, and I can see them carrying on with life, and I can hear it, but I can’t quite join in the way I used to.

So yes, I’d say it has caused problems. Maybe not open conflict, exactly, but distance. And I think people notice that I’m not really there with them in the same way.
